Just… tired and kind of blah maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s like my mind doesn’t really stay on one thing, it just jumps around. Money, mostly, and then we’ve got a relative staying with us, so I’m thinking about that, and whether my wife is stressed about it. Then I’ll remember something I’m supposed to do for church, and then suddenly I’m back to wondering if I messed something up at work.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s just… one thing after another. Like my brain keeps making a list I didn’t ask for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly falling asleep, I think, but also I’ll wake up and it’s like I never fully shut off.\n\nI’ll get in bed really tired, like I’m ready to just be out, and then my head starts going—bills, whether I wrote down my work schedule right, stuff going on at home, if everyone’s okay. And then I’ll remember some random thing, like we’re almost out of milk, which isn’t even a big deal but suddenly it feels like part of the whole pile.\n\nSo I do sleep, but a lot of mornings it doesn’t feel like I really settled down, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Just… tired and kind of blah maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s like my mind doesn’t really stay on one thing, it just jumps around. Money, mostly, and then we’ve got a relative staying with us, so I’m thinking about that, and whether my wife is stressed about it. Then I’ll remember something I’m supposed to do for church, and then suddenly I’m back to wondering if I messed something up at work.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s just… one thing after another. Like my brain keeps making a list I didn’t ask for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly falling asleep, I think, but also I’ll wake up and it’s like I never fully shut off.\n\nI’ll get in bed really tired, like I’m ready to just be out, and then my head starts going—bills, whether I wrote down my work schedule right, stuff going on at home, if everyone’s okay. And then I’ll remember some random thing, like we’re almost out of milk, which isn’t even a big deal but suddenly it feels like part of the whole pile.\n\nSo I do sleep, but a lot of mornings it doesn’t feel like I really settled down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about a year and a few months. Maybe a year and three months, something like that. It started around when our older relative moved in with us.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the adjustment, you know, moving furniture around and figuring out routines and all that. We had to clear out space, and I remember thinking the hallway felt crowded for weeks. But yeah… when I look back, that’s about when it started.\n\nDoctor: If your relative were no longer living with you, do you think you’d still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the relative being there definitely adds to it, with the house feeling busier and all that, but it’s not just that one thing.\n\nMy mind kind of bounces around no matter what—money, whether my wife is stressed, church stuff I said I’d help with, if I’m doing something wrong at work. Even if the house got quieter, I feel like my brain would just pick the next thing and start worrying about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense, like your muscles are tight or you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to my muscles like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of worn out, but I’m on my feet at the register a lot, and there’s this one mat they put down that’s supposed to help but it kind of curls up at the edge—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI’m not sure I’d say tight exactly. Maybe I just don’t know what counts as tense.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’ll tell myself, like, “Just pray on it and let it go,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right now,” but it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like once one worry gets started, it kind of rolls into the next thing. I’ll be thinking about one small thing, and then suddenly I’m running through every bad way the whole day could go. Like I’m trying to plan for problems that haven’t even happened. And then I realize I’ve been stuck on it for a while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

And at work, standing there scanning stuff, people buying the same things every morning—coffee, gum, those little scratch tickets—it just feels like I’m moving through mud. So… yeah, mostly I’m just tired all the time, and I figured I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not depressed exactly, I guess. Like, I’m not walking around crying or anything. It’s more like I feel worn down, if that makes sense.\n\nBut “down” is kind of hard to say, because some days at work, you know, if someone comes through my line and they’re rude over a coupon or whatever, it sticks with me longer than it probably should. Then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m making dinner, and then I’m thinking about how I forgot to buy dish soap… so, I don’t know. I wouldn’t really call it depressed. Just… tired and kind of blah maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s like my mind doesn’t really stay on one thing, it just jumps around. Like my brain keeps making a list I didn’t ask for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly falling asleep, I think, but also I’ll wake up and it’s like I never fully shut off.\n\nI’ll get in bed really tired, like I’m ready to just be out, and then my head starts going—bills, whether I wrote down my work schedule right, stuff going on at home, if everyone’s okay. Like sometimes my wife will ask if I’m quiet and I’ll say I’m fine, and then I start thinking about whether I sounded rude when I said it—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed feeling depressed most of the day, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been snapping at people all the time or anything.\n\nMaybe I get a little short sometimes, but then I wonder if that’s just normal, like if the line at work is backed up and somebody’s coupons aren’t scanning right—sorry, that’s kind of specific. I guess I haven’t noticed being more irritable than usual, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating because your mind keeps getting pulled into worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. Like at the register, I can be looking right at the screen, and then my mind kind of goes off to stuff at home or whether a bill is coming due, and then I realize I lost track.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, but I’ll have to recount the drawer or double-check because I catch some small mistake. And then of course I start thinking, well, did I mess up because I was worried, or was it just a busy shift… but yeah, worry does pull my attention sometimes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’m pacing around the house or anything like that.

Sometimes after work I feel kind of wound up, but then again, standing at a register all day is weird because you’re tired but also still sort of buzzing from people asking questions and the beeping and all that. I don’t know if that counts as keyed up.

So… I haven’t really noticed it being a big thing, I guess.

It’s more like I feel worn down, if that makes sense.\n\nBut “down” is kind of hard to say, because some days at work, you know, if someone comes through my line and they’re rude over a coupon or whatever, it sticks with me longer than it probably should. Then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m making dinner, and then I’m thinking about how I forgot to buy dish soap… so, I don’t know. I wouldn’t really call it depressed. And then I’ll remember some random thing, like we’re almost out of milk, which isn’t even a big deal but suddenly it feels like part of the whole pile.\n\nSo I do sleep, but a lot of mornings it doesn’t feel like I really settled down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about a year and a few months. I mean, I don’t think I’m pacing around the house or anything like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of wound up, but then again, standing at a register all day is weird because you’re tired but also still sort of buzzing from people asking questions and the beeping and all that. I don’t know if that counts as keyed up.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed it being a big thing, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nIn the past two weeks, have you felt guilty or worthless a lot?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty or worthless… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad sometimes if I mess something up, like if my drawer’s off or I forget something small, but I don’t think I’d call it worthless.

That word feels pretty strong. I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling that way a lot. I’m probably more hard on myself than I should be, but… not like constantly guilty or anything.

And at work, standing there scanning stuff, people buying the same things every morning—coffee, gum, those little scratch tickets—it just feels like I’m moving through mud. So… yeah, mostly I’m just tired all the time, and I figured I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not depressed exactly, I guess. Like, I’m not walking around crying or anything. It’s more like I feel worn down, if that makes sense.\n\nBut “down” is kind of hard to say, because some days at work, you know, if someone comes through my line and they’re rude over a coupon or whatever, it sticks with me longer than it probably should. Then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m making dinner, and then I’m thinking about how I forgot to buy dish soap… so, I don’t know. I wouldn’t really call it depressed. Just… tired and kind of blah maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s like my mind doesn’t really stay on one thing, it just jumps around. Money, mostly, and then we’ve got a relative staying with us, so I’m thinking about that, and whether my wife is stressed about it. Then I’ll remember something I’m supposed to do for church, and then suddenly I’m back to wondering if I messed something up at work.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s just… one thing after another. Like my brain keeps making a list I didn’t ask for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly falling asleep, I think, but also I’ll wake up and it’s like I never fully shut off.\n\nI’ll get in bed really tired, like I’m ready to just be out, and then my head starts going—bills, whether I wrote down my work schedule right, stuff going on at home, if everyone’s okay. And then I’ll remember some random thing, like we’re almost out of milk, which isn’t even a big deal but suddenly it feels like part of the whole pile.\n\nSo I do sleep, but a lot of mornings it doesn’t feel like I really settled down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about a year and a few months. Maybe a year and three months, something like that. It started around when our older relative moved in with us.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the adjustment, you know, moving furniture around and figuring out routines and all that. We had to clear out space, and I remember thinking the hallway felt crowded for weeks. But yeah… when I look back, that’s about when it started.\n\nDoctor: If your relative were no longer living with you, do you think you’d still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the relative being there definitely adds to it, with the house feeling busier and all that, but it’s not just that one thing.\n\nMy mind kind of bounces around no matter what—money, whether my wife is stressed, church stuff I said I’d help with, if I’m doing something wrong at work. Even if the house got quieter, I feel like my brain would just pick the next thing and start worrying about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense, like your muscles are tight or you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to my muscles like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of worn out, but I’m on my feet at the register a lot, and there’s this one mat they put down that’s supposed to help but it kind of curls up at the edge—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI’m not sure I’d say tight exactly. Maybe I just don’t know what counts as tense.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’ll tell myself, like, “Just pray on it and let it go,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right now,” but it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like once one worry gets started, it kind of rolls into the next thing. I’ll be thinking about one small thing, and then suddenly I’m running through every bad way the whole day could go. Like I’m trying to plan for problems that haven’t even happened. And then I realize I’ve been stuck on it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I usually like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost interest.”\n\nLike, I watch shows with my wife sometimes, or I’ll go to church things, but then my mind wanders and I start thinking about what I need to do after. Although that happens even when the show isn’t that good, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed not enjoying things… but I also haven’t been paying real close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’m at work and I’m just scanning groceries and saying the same things to people, and it’s hard to tell if I’m actually down or just tired of the routine.\n\nHopeless… that sounds strong. I don’t think I’ve really sat there thinking that. But I also don’t always stop and check in with myself, you know? Like sometimes my wife will ask if I’m quiet and I’ll say I’m fine, and then I start thinking about whether I sounded rude when I said it—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed feeling depressed most of the day, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been snapping at people all the time or anything.\n\nMaybe I get a little short sometimes, but then I wonder if that’s just normal, like if the line at work is backed up and somebody’s coupons aren’t scanning right—sorry, that’s kind of specific. I guess I haven’t noticed being more irritable than usual, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating because your mind keeps getting pulled into worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. Like at the register, I can be looking right at the screen, and then my mind kind of goes off to stuff at home or whether a bill is coming due, and then I realize I lost track.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, but I’ll have to recount the drawer or double-check because I catch some small mistake. And then of course I start thinking, well, did I mess up because I was worried, or was it just a busy shift… but yeah, worry does pull my attention sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I don’t know if that counts as keyed up.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed it being a big thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt guilty or worthless a lot?\n\nPatient: Guilty or worthless… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad sometimes if I mess something up, like if my drawer’s off or I forget something small, but I don’t think I’d call it worthless.\n\nThat word feels pretty strong. I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling that way a lot. I’m probably more hard on myself than I should be, but… not like constantly guilty or anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas this worrying been causing problems in your daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I've had to double-check my cash drawer more often because of small mistakes, and I've cut back on volunteering at the food pantry because I don't feel like I can keep up with everything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, a little. I mean, I’m still getting through my day, going to work and all that, but it’s caused some problems.

Like at work, I’ve been double-checking my cash drawer more than I used to because I’ll make these small mistakes, or think I did, and then I don’t trust it. And outside work… I used to help out at the food pantry, not every day or anything, but I’ve cut back on that because I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything. Then I feel bad because they’re nice people there, and one of the guys always asks about my truck—well, it’s not really a truck, it’s this old hatchback—but yeah. I guess the worrying has made me pull back some.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. Like, I’ll get a full night’s sleep, or what seems like one, and then when I get up for my cashier shift my body and my head are still dragging.\n\nIt’s weird because I used to be able to just get up and go, you know? And at work, standing there scanning stuff, people buying the same things every morning—coffee, gum, those little scratch tickets—it just feels like I’m moving through mud. So… yeah, mostly I’m just tired all the time, and I figured I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not depressed exactly, I guess. Like, I’m not walking around crying or anything. It’s more like I feel worn down, if that makes sense.\n\nBut “down” is kind of hard to say, because some days at work, you know, if someone comes through my line and they’re rude over a coupon or whatever, it sticks with me longer than it probably should. Then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m making dinner, and then I’m thinking about how I forgot to buy dish soap… so, I don’t know. I wouldn’t really call it depressed. Just… tired and kind of blah maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s like my mind doesn’t really stay on one thing, it just jumps around. Money, mostly, and then we’ve got a relative staying with us, so I’m thinking about that, and whether my wife is stressed about it. Then I’ll remember something I’m supposed to do for church, and then suddenly I’m back to wondering if I messed something up at work.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s just… one thing after another. Like my brain keeps making a list I didn’t ask for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly falling asleep, I think, but also I’ll wake up and it’s like I never fully shut off.\n\nI’ll get in bed really tired, like I’m ready to just be out, and then my head starts going—bills, whether I wrote down my work schedule right, stuff going on at home, if everyone’s okay. And then I’ll remember some random thing, like we’re almost out of milk, which isn’t even a big deal but suddenly it feels like part of the whole pile.\n\nSo I do sleep, but a lot of mornings it doesn’t feel like I really settled down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about a year and a few months. Maybe a year and three months, something like that. It started around when our older relative moved in with us.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the adjustment, you know, moving furniture around and figuring out routines and all that. We had to clear out space, and I remember thinking the hallway felt crowded for weeks. But yeah… when I look back, that’s about when it started.\n\nDoctor: If your relative were no longer living with you, do you think you’d still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the relative being there definitely adds to it, with the house feeling busier and all that, but it’s not just that one thing.\n\nMy mind kind of bounces around no matter what—money, whether my wife is stressed, church stuff I said I’d help with, if I’m doing something wrong at work. Even if the house got quieter, I feel like my brain would just pick the next thing and start worrying about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense, like your muscles are tight or you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to my muscles like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of worn out, but I’m on my feet at the register a lot, and there’s this one mat they put down that’s supposed to help but it kind of curls up at the edge—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI’m not sure I’d say tight exactly. Maybe I just don’t know what counts as tense.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’ll tell myself, like, “Just pray on it and let it go,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right now,” but it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like once one worry gets started, it kind of rolls into the next thing. I’ll be thinking about one small thing, and then suddenly I’m running through every bad way the whole day could go. Like I’m trying to plan for problems that haven’t even happened. And then I realize I’ve been stuck on it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I usually like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost interest.”\n\nLike, I watch shows with my wife sometimes, or I’ll go to church things, but then my mind wanders and I start thinking about what I need to do after. Although that happens even when the show isn’t that good, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed not enjoying things… but I also haven’t been paying real close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’m at work and I’m just scanning groceries and saying the same things to people, and it’s hard to tell if I’m actually down or just tired of the routine.\n\nHopeless… that sounds strong. I don’t think I’ve really sat there thinking that. But I also don’t always stop and check in with myself, you know? Like sometimes my wife will ask if I’m quiet and I’ll say I’m fine, and then I start thinking about whether I sounded rude when I said it—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed feeling depressed most of the day, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been snapping at people all the time or anything.\n\nMaybe I get a little short sometimes, but then I wonder if that’s just normal, like if the line at work is backed up and somebody’s coupons aren’t scanning right—sorry, that’s kind of specific. I guess I haven’t noticed being more irritable than usual, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating because your mind keeps getting pulled into worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. Like at the register, I can be looking right at the screen, and then my mind kind of goes off to stuff at home or whether a bill is coming due, and then I realize I lost track.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, but I’ll have to recount the drawer or double-check because I catch some small mistake. And then of course I start thinking, well, did I mess up because I was worried, or was it just a busy shift… but yeah, worry does pull my attention sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’m pacing around the house or anything like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of wound up, but then again, standing at a register all day is weird because you’re tired but also still sort of buzzing from people asking questions and the beeping and all that. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. Like, I’ll get a full night’s sleep, or what seems like one, and then when I get up for my cashier shift my body and my head are still dragging.\n\nIt’s weird because I used to be able to just get up and go, you know? And at work, standing there scanning stuff, people buying the same things every morning—coffee, gum, those little scratch tickets—it just feels like I’m moving through mud. So… yeah, mostly I’m just tired all the time, and I figured I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not depressed exactly, I guess. Like, I’m not walking around crying or anything. It’s more like I feel worn down, if that makes sense.\n\nBut “down” is kind of hard to say, because some days at work, you know, if someone comes through my line and they’re rude over a coupon or whatever, it sticks with me longer than it probably should. Then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m making dinner, and then I’m thinking about how I forgot to buy dish soap… so, I don’t know. I wouldn’t really call it depressed. Just… tired and kind of blah maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s like my mind doesn’t really stay on one thing, it just jumps around. Money, mostly, and then we’ve got a relative staying with us, so I’m thinking about that, and whether my wife is stressed about it. Then I’ll remember something I’m supposed to do for church, and then suddenly I’m back to wondering if I messed something up at work.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s just… one thing after another. Like my brain keeps making a list I didn’t ask for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly falling asleep, I think, but also I’ll wake up and it’s like I never fully shut off.\n\nI’ll get in bed really tired, like I’m ready to just be out, and then my head starts going—bills, whether I wrote down my work schedule right, stuff going on at home, if everyone’s okay. And then I’ll remember some random thing, like we’re almost out of milk, which isn’t even a big deal but suddenly it feels like part of the whole pile.\n\nSo I do sleep, but a lot of mornings it doesn’t feel like I really settled down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about a year and a few months. Maybe a year and three months, something like that. It started around when our older relative moved in with us.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the adjustment, you know, moving furniture around and figuring out routines and all that. We had to clear out space, and I remember thinking the hallway felt crowded for weeks. But yeah… when I look back, that’s about when it started.\n\nDoctor: If your relative were no longer living with you, do you think you’d still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the relative being there definitely adds to it, with the house feeling busier and all that, but it’s not just that one thing.\n\nMy mind kind of bounces around no matter what—money, whether my wife is stressed, church stuff I said I’d help with, if I’m doing something wrong at work. Even if the house got quieter, I feel like my brain would just pick the next thing and start worrying about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense, like your muscles are tight or you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to my muscles like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of worn out, but I’m on my feet at the register a lot, and there’s this one mat they put down that’s supposed to help but it kind of curls up at the edge—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI’m not sure I’d say tight exactly. Maybe I just don’t know what counts as tense.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’ll tell myself, like, “Just pray on it and let it go,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right now,” but it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like once one worry gets started, it kind of rolls into the next thing. I’ll be thinking about one small thing, and then suddenly I’m running through every bad way the whole day could go. Like I’m trying to plan for problems that haven’t even happened. And then I realize I’ve been stuck on it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I usually like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost interest.”\n\nLike, I watch shows with my wife sometimes, or I’ll go to church things, but then my mind wanders and I start thinking about what I need to do after. Although that happens even when the show isn’t that good, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed not enjoying things… but I also haven’t been paying real close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’m at work and I’m just scanning groceries and saying the same things to people, and it’s hard to tell if I’m actually down or just tired of the routine.\n\nHopeless… that sounds strong. I don’t think I’ve really sat there thinking that. But I also don’t always stop and check in with myself, you know? Like sometimes my wife will ask if I’m quiet and I’ll say I’m fine, and then I start thinking about whether I sounded rude when I said it—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed feeling depressed most of the day, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been snapping at people all the time or anything.\n\nMaybe I get a little short sometimes, but then I wonder if that’s just normal, like if the line at work is backed up and somebody’s coupons aren’t scanning right—sorry, that’s kind of specific. I guess I haven’t noticed being more irritable than usual, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating because your mind keeps getting pulled into worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. Like at the register, I can be looking right at the screen, and then my mind kind of goes off to stuff at home or whether a bill is coming due, and then I realize I lost track.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, but I’ll have to recount the drawer or double-check because I catch some small mistake. And then of course I start thinking, well, did I mess up because I was worried, or was it just a busy shift… but yeah, worry does pull my attention sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. And at work, standing there scanning stuff, people buying the same things every morning—coffee, gum, those little scratch tickets—it just feels like I’m moving through mud. So… yeah, mostly I’m just tired all the time, and I figured I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not depressed exactly, I guess. Like, I’m not walking around crying or anything. It’s more like I feel worn down, if that makes sense.\n\nBut “down” is kind of hard to say, because some days at work, you know, if someone comes through my line and they’re rude over a coupon or whatever, it sticks with me longer than it probably should. Then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m making dinner, and then I’m thinking about how I forgot to buy dish soap… so, I don’t know. I wouldn’t really call it depressed. Just… tired and kind of blah maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s like my mind doesn’t really stay on one thing, it just jumps around. Money, mostly, and then we’ve got a relative staying with us, so I’m thinking about that, and whether my wife is stressed about it. Then I’ll remember something I’m supposed to do for church, and then suddenly I’m back to wondering if I messed something up at work.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s just… one thing after another. Like my brain keeps making a list I didn’t ask for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly falling asleep, I think, but also I’ll wake up and it’s like I never fully shut off.\n\nI’ll get in bed really tired, like I’m ready to just be out, and then my head starts going—bills, whether I wrote down my work schedule right, stuff going on at home, if everyone’s okay. And then I’ll remember some random thing, like we’re almost out of milk, which isn’t even a big deal but suddenly it feels like part of the whole pile.\n\nSo I do sleep, but a lot of mornings it doesn’t feel like I really settled down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about a year and a few months. Maybe a year and three months, something like that. It started around when our older relative moved in with us.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the adjustment, you know, moving furniture around and figuring out routines and all that. We had to clear out space, and I remember thinking the hallway felt crowded for weeks. But yeah… when I look back, that’s about when it started.\n\nDoctor: If your relative were no longer living with you, do you think you’d still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the relative being there definitely adds to it, with the house feeling busier and all that, but it’s not just that one thing.\n\nMy mind kind of bounces around no matter what—money, whether my wife is stressed, church stuff I said I’d help with, if I’m doing something wrong at work. Even if the house got quieter, I feel like my brain would just pick the next thing and start worrying about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense, like your muscles are tight or you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to my muscles like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of worn out, but I’m on my feet at the register a lot, and there’s this one mat they put down that’s supposed to help but it kind of curls up at the edge—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI’m not sure I’d say tight exactly. Maybe I just don’t know what counts as tense.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’ll tell myself, like, “Just pray on it and let it go,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right now,” but it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like once one worry gets started, it kind of rolls into the next thing. I’ll be thinking about one small thing, and then suddenly I’m running through every bad way the whole day could go. Like I’m trying to plan for problems that haven’t even happened. And then I realize I’ve been stuck on it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I usually like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost interest.”\n\nLike, I watch shows with my wife sometimes, or I’ll go to church things, but then my mind wanders and I start thinking about what I need to do after. Although that happens even when the show isn’t that good, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed not enjoying things… but I also haven’t been paying real close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’m at work and I’m just scanning groceries and saying the same things to people, and it’s hard to tell if I’m actually down or just tired of the routine.\n\nHopeless… that sounds strong. I don’t think I’ve really sat there thinking that. But I also don’t always stop and check in with myself, you know? Like sometimes my wife will ask if I’m quiet and I’ll say I’m fine, and then I start thinking about whether I sounded rude when I said it—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed feeling depressed most of the day, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been snapping at people all the time or anything.\n\nMaybe I get a little short sometimes, but then I wonder if that’s just normal, like if the line at work is backed up and somebody’s coupons aren’t scanning right—sorry, that’s kind of specific. I guess I haven’t noticed being more irritable than usual, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating because your mind keeps getting pulled into worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. Like at the register, I can be looking right at the screen, and then my mind kind of goes off to stuff at home or whether a bill is coming due, and then I realize I lost track.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, but I’ll have to recount the drawer or double-check because I catch some small mistake. And then of course I start thinking, well, did I mess up because I was worried, or was it just a busy shift… but yeah, worry does pull my attention sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’m pacing around the house or anything like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of wound up, but then again, standing at a register all day is weird because you’re tired but also still sort of buzzing from people asking questions and the beeping and all that. I don’t know if that counts as keyed up.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed it being a big thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt guilty or worthless a lot?\n\nPatient: Guilty or worthless… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad sometimes if I mess something up, like if my drawer’s off or I forget something small, but I don’t think I’d call it worthless.\n\nThat word feels pretty strong. I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling that way a lot. I’m probably more hard on myself than I should be, but… not like constantly guilty or anything.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been causing problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. Then I’ll remember something I’m supposed to do for church, and then suddenly I’m back to wondering if I messed something up at work.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s just… one thing after another. Like my brain keeps making a list I didn’t ask for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly falling asleep, I think, but also I’ll wake up and it’s like I never fully shut off.\n\nI’ll get in bed really tired, like I’m ready to just be out, and then my head starts going—bills, whether I wrote down my work schedule right, stuff going on at home, if everyone’s okay. And then I’ll remember some random thing, like we’re almost out of milk, which isn’t even a big deal but suddenly it feels like part of the whole pile.\n\nSo I do sleep, but a lot of mornings it doesn’t feel like I really settled down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about a year and a few months. Maybe a year and three months, something like that. It started around when our older relative moved in with us.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the adjustment, you know, moving furniture around and figuring out routines and all that. We had to clear out space, and I remember thinking the hallway felt crowded for weeks. But yeah… when I look back, that’s about when it started.\n\nDoctor: If your relative were no longer living with you, do you think you’d still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the relative being there definitely adds to it, with the house feeling busier and all that, but it’s not just that one thing.\n\nMy mind kind of bounces around no matter what—money, whether my wife is stressed, church stuff I said I’d help with, if I’m doing something wrong at work. Even if the house got quieter, I feel like my brain would just pick the next thing and start worrying about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense, like your muscles are tight or you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to my muscles like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of worn out, but I’m on my feet at the register a lot, and there’s this one mat they put down that’s supposed to help but it kind of curls up at the edge—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI’m not sure I’d say tight exactly. Maybe I just don’t know what counts as tense.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’ll tell myself, like, “Just pray on it and let it go,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right now,” but it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like once one worry gets started, it kind of rolls into the next thing. I’ll be thinking about one small thing, and then suddenly I’m running through every bad way the whole day could go. Like I’m trying to plan for problems that haven’t even happened. And then I realize I’ve been stuck on it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I usually like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost interest.”\n\nLike, I watch shows with my wife sometimes, or I’ll go to church things, but then my mind wanders and I start thinking about what I need to do after. Although that happens even when the show isn’t that good, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed not enjoying things… but I also haven’t been paying real close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’m at work and I’m just scanning groceries and saying the same things to people, and it’s hard to tell if I’m actually down or just tired of the routine.\n\nHopeless… that sounds strong. I don’t think I’ve really sat there thinking that. But I also don’t always stop and check in with myself, you know? Like sometimes my wife will ask if I’m quiet and I’ll say I’m fine, and then I start thinking about whether I sounded rude when I said it—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed feeling depressed most of the day, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been snapping at people all the time or anything.\n\nMaybe I get a little short sometimes, but then I wonder if that’s just normal, like if the line at work is backed up and somebody’s coupons aren’t scanning right—sorry, that’s kind of specific. I guess I haven’t noticed being more irritable than usual, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating because your mind keeps getting pulled into worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. Like at the register, I can be looking right at the screen, and then my mind kind of goes off to stuff at home or whether a bill is coming due, and then I realize I lost track.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, but I’ll have to recount the drawer or double-check because I catch some small mistake. And then of course I start thinking, well, did I mess up because I was worried, or was it just a busy shift… but yeah, worry does pull my attention sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’m pacing around the house or anything like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of wound up, but then again, standing at a register all day is weird because you’re tired but also still sort of buzzing from people asking questions and the beeping and all that. I don’t know if that counts as keyed up.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed it being a big thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt guilty or worthless a lot?\n\nPatient: Guilty or worthless… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad sometimes if I mess something up, like if my drawer’s off or I forget something small, but I don’t think I’d call it worthless.\n\nThat word feels pretty strong. I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling that way a lot. I’m probably more hard on myself than I should be, but… not like constantly guilty or anything.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been causing problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. I mean, I’m still getting through my day, going to work and all that, but it’s caused some problems.\n\nLike at work, I’ve been double-checking my cash drawer more than I used to because I’ll make these small mistakes, or think I did, and then I don’t trust it. And outside work… I used to help out at the food pantry, not every day or anything, but I’ve cut back on that because I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything. Then I feel bad because they’re nice people there, and one of the guys always asks about my truck—well, it’s not really a truck, it’s this old hatchback—but yeah. I guess the worrying has made me pull back some.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about your relative living with you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just about my relative staying with us. That’s part of it, sure, but my mind kind of jumps around.\n\nI’ll be thinking about money, then whether my wife is stressed, then something at church I said I’d do, then whether I messed something up at work. Like the other day I was trying to remember if I’d signed up to help with something, and then somehow I’m thinking about the light bill and my cash drawer at the same time.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s a bunch of different things, not just one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic or wired for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Unusually energetic or wired for several days… I don’t think so. I mean, I get worked up sometimes, like if there’s a lot going on or I’ve had too much coffee—though honestly the coffee at work is terrible, it just sits there getting burnt—but not like days in a row where I’m full of energy or anything.\n\nIf anything, I usually just feel kind of tense, not energized. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you usually feel refreshed?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can sleep a full night and still wake up feeling like my body hasn’t caught up. My head feels kind of heavy too, like I’m already dragging before I even get to my cashier shift.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I thought maybe I was just getting older, but I’m 35, not 80—though my knees make a weird noise sometimes when I’m getting into the car. But yeah, mornings… I usually don’t feel refreshed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I get worked up sometimes, like if there’s a lot going on or I’ve had too much coffee—though honestly the coffee at work is terrible, it just sits there getting burnt—but not like days in a row where I’m full of energy or anything.\n\nIf anything, I usually just feel kind of tense, not energized. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you usually feel refreshed?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can sleep a full night and still wake up feeling like my body hasn’t caught up. But yeah… when I look back, that’s about when it started.\n\nDoctor: If your relative were no longer living with you, do you think you’d still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the relative being there definitely adds to it, with the house feeling busier and all that, but it’s not just that one thing.\n\nMy mind kind of bounces around no matter what—money, whether my wife is stressed, church stuff I said I’d help with, if I’m doing something wrong at work. Even if the house got quieter, I feel like my brain would just pick the next thing and start worrying about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense, like your muscles are tight or you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to my muscles like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of worn out, but I’m on my feet at the register a lot, and there’s this one mat they put down that’s supposed to help but it kind of curls up at the edge—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI’m not sure I’d say tight exactly. Maybe I just don’t know what counts as tense.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I’ll tell myself, like, “Just pray on it and let it go,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right now,” but it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like once one worry gets started, it kind of rolls into the next thing. I’ll be thinking about one small thing, and then suddenly I’m running through every bad way the whole day could go. Like I’m trying to plan for problems that haven’t even happened. And then I realize I’ve been stuck on it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I usually like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost interest.”\n\nLike, I watch shows with my wife sometimes, or I’ll go to church things, but then my mind wanders and I start thinking about what I need to do after. Although that happens even when the show isn’t that good, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed not enjoying things… but I also haven’t been paying real close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’m at work and I’m just scanning groceries and saying the same things to people, and it’s hard to tell if I’m actually down or just tired of the routine.\n\nHopeless… that sounds strong. I don’t think I’ve really sat there thinking that. But I also don’t always stop and check in with myself, you know? Like sometimes my wife will ask if I’m quiet and I’ll say I’m fine, and then I start thinking about whether I sounded rude when I said it—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed feeling depressed most of the day, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been snapping at people all the time or anything.\n\nMaybe I get a little short sometimes, but then I wonder if that’s just normal, like if the line at work is backed up and somebody’s coupons aren’t scanning right—sorry, that’s kind of specific. I guess I haven’t noticed being more irritable than usual, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating because your mind keeps getting pulled into worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. Like at the register, I can be looking right at the screen, and then my mind kind of goes off to stuff at home or whether a bill is coming due, and then I realize I lost track.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, but I’ll have to recount the drawer or double-check because I catch some small mistake. And then of course I start thinking, well, did I mess up because I was worried, or was it just a busy shift… but yeah, worry does pull my attention sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’m pacing around the house or anything like that.\n\nSometimes after work I feel kind of wound up, but then again, standing at a register all day is weird because you’re tired but also still sort of buzzing from people asking questions and the beeping and all that. I don’t know if that counts as keyed up.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed it being a big thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt guilty or worthless a lot?\n\nPatient: Guilty or worthless… I don’t know. I mean, I feel bad sometimes if I mess something up, like if my drawer’s off or I forget something small, but I don’t think I’d call it worthless.\n\nThat word feels pretty strong. I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling that way a lot. I’m probably more hard on myself than I should be, but… not like constantly guilty or anything.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been causing problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. I mean, I’m still getting through my day, going to work and all that, but it’s caused some problems.\n\nLike at work, I’ve been double-checking my cash drawer more than I used to because I’ll make these small mistakes, or think I did, and then I don’t trust it. And outside work… I used to help out at the food pantry, not every day or anything, but I’ve cut back on that because I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything. Then I feel bad because they’re nice people there, and one of the guys always asks about my truck—well, it’s not really a truck, it’s this old hatchback—but yeah. I guess the worrying has made me pull back some.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about your relative living with you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just about my relative staying with us. That’s part of it, sure, but my mind kind of jumps around.\n\nI’ll be thinking about money, then whether my wife is stressed, then something at church I said I’d do, then whether I messed something up at work. Like the other day I was trying to remember if I’d signed up to help with something, and then somehow I’m thinking about the light bill and my cash drawer at the same time.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s a bunch of different things, not just one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic or wired for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Unusually energetic or wired for several days… I don’t think so. I mean, I get worked up sometimes, like if there’s a lot going on or I’ve had too much coffee—though honestly the coffee at work is terrible, it just sits there getting burnt—but not like days in a row where I’m full of energy or anything.\n\nIf anything, I usually just feel kind of tense, not energized. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you usually feel refreshed?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can sleep a full night and still wake up feeling like my body hasn’t caught up. My head feels kind of heavy too, like I’m already dragging before I even get to my cashier shift.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I thought maybe I was just getting older, but I’m 35, not 80—though my knees make a weird noise sometimes when I’m getting into the car. But yeah, mornings… I usually don’t feel refreshed.\n\nDoctor: Do you snore loudly?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. My wife hasn’t made a big deal about it, I don’t think. I mean, sometimes she’ll nudge me if I’m taking up too much space or the blanket’s all twisted—somehow I always end up with the sheet halfway off the bed—but I don’t know if that’s because of snoring.\n\nSo… loud snoring? I haven’t really noticed, and nobody’s told me that straight out.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife ever told you that you stop breathing in your sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think she’s ever said that. I mean, she’s complained about me stealing the pillow—apparently I “migrate” across the bed, which I don’t even know how that happens when I’m asleep—but stopping breathing, no, I don’t remember her saying anything like that.\n\nI guess I could ask her specifically, but as far as I know, she hasn’t noticed that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
